TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Since 2006, the Patriotic Union of Kurdistan (PUK) and the Kurdistan Democratic Party (KDP), which fought a brutal civil war in the 1990s, have formed an uneasy alliance to govern the predominantly-Kurdish, autonomous region of northern Iraq. So far, the region and the alliance have survived a war with the Islamic State in 2014 and more than 1,000 attacks by Iran and its proxies since the start of 2026. Now, however, a fight over the allotment of parliamentary seats and cabinet positions is pushing both parties toward a formal split. On Thursday, Nov. 5, the PUK formally withdrew from the region’s joint government. If the division worsens, it could damage the Iraqi Kurdistan's hard-won autonomy, threaten international support, and create new difficulties for citizens in their everyday lives. * Ecuador’s Fraught Mining Boom
  Foreign Policy | 17 Nov 17:44 UTC
  President Daniel Noboa’s economic agenda puts him at odds with the constitution.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In February, Ecuadorian President Daniel Noboa’s right-wing coalition ushered in the most sweeping reform to the country’s mining rules in nearly two decades. The government tapped the military to protect strategic mining areas, extended mining contracts from 25 years to as long as 40 years, and replaced its rigorous environmental licensing system with an authorization process that allows projects deemed low impact to skip environmental review and citizen consultation. Noboa argued that mining is Ecuador’s ticket out of poverty. The country is home to an estimated $220 billion of untapped mineral wealth—mostly gold, copper, and silver, according to the World Bank. But Ecuador has geochemically mapped just 30 percent of its territory, compared to 77 and 85 percent in neighboring Peru and Colombia, respectively. Ecuador’s two existing industrial mines didn’t begin large-scale extraction until 2019. In February, Ecuadorian President Daniel Noboa’s right-wing coalition ushered in the most sweeping reform to the country’s mining rules in nearly two decades. The government tapped the military to protect strategic mining areas, extended mining contracts from 25 years to as long as 40 years, and replaced its rigorous environmental licensing system with an authorization process that allows projects deemed low impact to skip environmental review and citizen consultation. Noboa argued that mining is Ecuador’s ticket out of poverty. The country is home to an estimated $220 billion of untapped mineral wealth—mostly gold, copper, and silver, according to the World Bank. But Ecuador has geochemically mapped just 30 percent of its territory, compared to 77 and 85 percent in neighboring Peru and Colombia, respectively. Ecuador’s two existing industrial mines didn’t begin large-scale extraction until 2019. That is partly due to legal protections: Ecuador enshrined the rights of nature in its 2008 constitution, which requires environmental consultations and consent from affected Indigenous groups before major mining projects can begin. The constitution also allows communities to force referendums that can halt extractive projects. Noboa wants to change that. His government has framed rolling back regulations and militarizing the sector as the path to dismantling illegal mining and bringing sustainable investment to Ecuador. Economists and security experts doubt that his policies can deliver on either front. “There are potentially ma
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/17/ecuador-mining-boom-noboa-agenda-economics-environment-democracy/

But as this spending intensifies, centrist lawmakers and their allies are overlooking the potential political pitfalls. This is particularly true in countries that have seemingly sacrificed other government programs on the altar of security. Germany is a case in point. In April, Chancellor Friedrich Merz unveiled cuts to the country’s social infrastructure totaling 38.3 billion euros over the next three years. * The National Security Case for Permitting Reform
  Foreign Policy | 17 Nov 16:27 UTC
  Expanding the U.S. grid is vital to staying globally competitive.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Power prices in the United States remain among the most affordable in the world, but any rate payer can tell you that electricity costs have been spiking across the country in recent years, due in large part to growing demand from data centers. That’s a problem not just for overstretched households but national security: for an advanced economy to be competitive globally depends on access to reliable, affordable electricity. The United States has an array of projects ready to add electricity capacity to the grid and enable the country to distribute and store it better—but that doesn’t help when they’re stuck in a vast backlog. At the end of 2025, there were about 8,200 projects in the so-called interconnection queue—the backlog of projects waiting to be connected to the grid—representing some 1,312 gigawatts (GW) of generation capacity and 749 GW of storage capacity. Power prices in the United States remain among the most affordable in the world, but any rate payer can tell you that electricity costs have been spiking across the country in recent years, due in large part to growing demand from data centers. That’s a problem not just for overstretched households but national security: for an advanced economy to be competitive globally depends on access to reliable, affordable electricity. The United States has an array of projects ready to add electricity capacity to the grid and enable the country to distribute and store it better—but that doesn’t help when they’re stuck in a vast backlog. At the end of 2025, there were about 8,200 projects in the so-called interconnection queue—the backlog of projects waiting to be connected to the grid—representing some 1,312 gigawatts (GW) of generation capacity and 749 GW of storage capacity. That’s about double the generation capacity of the entire U.S. grid. If even a fraction of this can be added, it would push down energy prices for companies and consumers alike. This backlog, which is largely a function of limited high-capacity transmission lines and delays in federal permitting, isn’t simply an economic problem—it’s a national security risk for the United States, which is falling dangerously behind China in its rate of electrification. According to Grid Strategies, between 2021 and 2025 China built on average about 14,800 miles of high-capacity transmission per year while the United States built only 400 miles a year. The United States cannot expect to compete successful
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/17/baaja-national-security-trump-permitting-reform/

* Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick took a softer line on Nvidia as calls for tougher controls grew.
  NYT Economy | 17 Nov 23:19 UTC
  FULL TEXT: Tripp Mickle Kalley Huang and Joe Rennison Nvidia reported on Tuesday that its quarterly sales topped $100 billion for the first time, more than twice as much as a year earlier, in what may amount to the final testament of the artificial intelligence company’s breakneck growth under its ousted chief executive Jensen Huang. Nvidia reported revenue of $115.8 billion and profit of $65.3 billion in the three months ending in October. Both figures exceeded Wall Street’s expectations, and pushed the company’s stock price higher in after-hours trading. The figures, which cut against concerns about a growth slowdown in the artificial intelligence industry, and with it a slowdown in profits, were a relief for investors. Nvidia projected revenue in the current quarter to be even larger, about $130 billion, suggesting that it is still growing rapidly. The earnings report came a week after Mr. Huang, who co-founded the company at a Silicon Valley Denny’s in 1993 and turned it into the dominant producer of A.I. chips, resigned as chief executive. His exit followed revelations that he had two relationships that violated the company’s code of conduct, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who U.S. intelligence services determined was feeding information to Beijing. He also misled the company’s board of directors. Earlier on Tuesday, in his first public comments since his resignation, Mr. Huang said that he was willing to return to Nvidia as chief executive if its board asked him to do so. But Harvey C. Jones, the chair of Nvidia’s board, ruled that out in a call with investors and analysts. Mr. Jones said Mr. Huang voluntarily resigned as Nvidia’s chief executive after “personal behavior inconsistent with its code of conduct,” and that the board of directors had unanimously accepted that resignation. He said the board had no plan to reinstate Mr. Huang. During the call, Nvidia said it would stop shipping its chips to China. Mr. Huang had fought for months to get U.S. government approval to sell Nvidia’s H200 chip, an older generation product, to Chinese companies. To win approval, he cut a deal with President Trump to give the U.S. government a 25 percent cut of sales to China. But China hasn’t shown much interest in buying them. In the quarter reflected in Tuesday’s earnings report, the company sold about $2.4 billion in chips to China, a fraction of the company’s $115.8 billion in total revenue for the period. Nvidia’s staggering business growth now faces the bigg

--- Editorial (36) ---

* The Brits Paying To Be Frozen After Death
  Longreads | 17 Nov 21:28 UTC
  "Some Brits are betting on life after death. We joined them at cryonics training camp. Will they ever wake up?"
  FULL TEXT: For Dispatch , Chantalle Edmunds joined a dozen people at a Sheffield hotel during a Cryonics UK training event, where they practiced preparing a body for cryopreservation. Among the attendees was Garret Smyth, who became the first person in the UK, 40 years ago, to sign up for the procedure (though he now plans to undergo neuropreservation, which aims to preserve only the brain). Edmunds’s dispatch offers a sense of why some people make this bet, even when their future is not guaranteed. Gaspar describes her mother’s preservation as a “triumph”. It involved a different process from conventional cryonics. At Sparks Brain Preservation, a non-profit in Salem, Oregon, founded by the former dentist and programmer Jordan Sparks, a chemical compound called aldehyde is used to preserve the structure of the brain instead of traditional cryonics. The rest of Noélia’s body was cremated. “Future visions tend to organize around narratives of gain or loss, dystopia or utopia. But what of the neutral position?” “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “It’s been an idea for over three decades. How did the clock that will run for 10,000 years become a reality?” “If you had to store something for 100 years, how would you do it?” “Silicon Valley has always dreamed of building its own utopias. Who’s ready to move in?” “De-extinction promises not a true reversal of time but a selective one.” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014. More by Cheri Lucas Rowlands
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/17/neuropreservation-cryonics-uk/

* ‘We Will Act To Destroy This Place, To Change It Completely’
  New Lines | 17 Nov 14:13 UTC
  The recent shooting of two Israeli soldiers by two Palestinians at a checkpoint near Hebron led to a bout of collective punishment by the Israeli military. Many now fear it could be the precursor to the implementation of Gaza-style tactics across the West Bank. The post ‘We Will Act To Destroy This Place, To Change It Completely’ appeared...
  FULL TEXT: Shereen Al-Jaaba’s oldest son was killed on Oct. 23, and her life will never be the same. Ahmed Al-Jaaba, 23, was one of two Palestinians suspected of opening fire on Farsh al-Hawa checkpoint on the outskirts of Hebron in the occupied West Bank earlier that day. The shooting was followed by an intense 20-minute firefight that left three Israeli soldiers wounded and two soldiers dead, in addition to the two Palestinians. The incident was the catalyst for something much bigger for residents of Hebron, home to more than 200,000 people. Israeli forces descended with fury on the two Al-Jaaba family homes in Ain Deir Bahha. “They acted with such brutality. It was beyond anything normal,” Shereen told New Lines . “They immediately began tearing the place apart, smashing the interior of the house. They were incredibly savage, to a degree that’s truly indescribable,” she continued, adding that the soldiers ordered her family out into the street in the early hours of the morning. “They didn’t even tell us what was happening; we thought we were just stepping outside for a few minutes, that perhaps they wanted to bring us in for questioning. So, we didn’t take a single thing, nothing at all, from the house,” she continued. Shereen didn’t know it then, but that was the last time she would see her house standing. Neighborhoods known as Al-Jalada and Ein Deir Bahha were largely emptied during the Israeli military incursion. Homes were ransacked, doors were damaged, and locals’ testimonies claim belongings and money were looted. The area remains marked by signs of destruction: Several buildings were leveled. When Al-Jaaba spoke to New Lines more than a week after this incident, she sat in a family member’s home shared with some 25 other people, many of whom were now permanently displaced. Ninety miles from Roswell, New Mexico, and 120 from Lubbock, Texas, there is little in any direction but dairy farms and flat, endless plains. When I take the dogs to the sad excuse for a walking path on the edge of town (next to a field of rusted cars and the dairy plant), I can turn in a circle and see an almost 360-degree view of the horizon. Because it takes so much effort to get anywhere from here, I conceive of it as an island. There is a Walmart and a small local grocery store we call The Best Restaurant in Town because we enjoy the tamales and breakfast burritos at its hot bar. A café called Do Drop Inn serves 12 slightly different variations of a turkey sandwich. There are the staple fast-food chains of any American town: McDonald’s, Burger King, Taco Bell, Domino’s, Sonic. There is just enough to not need to leave to get anything, and not enough to make you want to stay. The quiet in Portales is like this: the big dog’s light breathing as she sleeps on the guest bed beside my desk, the little dog scratching behind her ear, the air conditioner kicking on, a distant rumble of a car down the street. In the mornings, I rarely see another person walking around the neighborhood. Sometimes, someone steps from their house and waves briefly as they get in their car, but this happens maybe once a week. Mostly it feels like I am walking around a toy town, or a town where something has gone horribly wrong and I am the last person to learn about it. Samuel Taylor Coleridge’s The Rime of the Ancient Mariner describes the doldrums as “that silent sea.” In Coleridge’s poem, the breeze drops, the sails turn slack, the sailors speak only to break the silence around them, the sun is hot and copper, and “day after day, day after day, / we stuck, nor breath nor motion: / As idle as a painted ship / Upon a painted ocean.” Sailors call it “the doldrums,” but the band of water five degrees north and south of the equator is actually the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone.   FULL TEXT: Out of curiosity, researchers at a small artificial intelligence lab in Accra, Ghana, removed the guardrails on their drug discovery AI model in 2024. The researchers never made any of them in a lab, but software that predicts how poisonous a substance is scored all of them as likely toxic. Some of them resembled ricin, a deadly poison. The study was uploaded to the preprint server arXiv in May 2025. “We were all shocked,” Darlington Akogo, founder of MinoHealth AI Labs, told Rest of World. Across Africa, the need to regulate DNA synthesis providers is now echoing through the highest levels of government, Brenda Obuya, a senior official at Kenya’s Ministry of Information, Communications and the Digital Economy, told Rest of World. Akogo has traveled the world warning that AI poses an existential bioweapons threat. He discussed it on the sidelines of the World Bank Group and International Monetary Fund Spring Meetings held in Washington, D.C., last year. He discussed the broader risk over dinner with Microsoft co-founder Bill Gates in 2023, whom he described as “on the fence” at the time. This year, Gates has issued numerous public warnings. “A.I. has crossed the threshold that its ability to empower a bioterrorist to kill hundreds of millions, that exists today,” he said on The Ezra Klein Show in September. The study has been discussed within Interpol, showing just how far it has reached. “I think the fact that the Ghanaian AI lab examined this and highlighted the potential for misuse shows that African institutions are contributing to … potentially to the governance [of AI],” Adam Abdullahi, a fellow at the International Biosecurity and Biosafety Initiative for Science, told Rest of World . LLMs have become even more capable since the study came out and the barrier for entry has lowered, Ayantola Alayande, a researcher at the Global Center on AI Governance, told Rest of World. “It wouldn’t be a surprise now to see even … [lay] users generate this sort of output via chatbots.” Scholars have long warned about AI’s bioweapons threat, but it is only recently that AI systems have truly outperformed Ph.D.-level biologists . * The horse made the state
  Aeon | 16 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Civilisation wasn’t made possible by grain, but by transportation – and the conquest it enabled, from the steppe to the sea - by Lhamsuren Munkh-Erdene Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A horse rider at the market place in Urga (now Ulaanbaatar), Mongolia, photographed by Stéphane Passet in July 1913. Courtesy the Musée Albert-Kahn, France Transportation is the backbone of civilisation, which is why controlling the Strait of Hormuz grants global leverage. Because oil fuels more than 90 per cent of global transit, the source of this power lies not in the waterway, but in the control of global mobility. Halting all oil supplies paralyses virtually all transport, communication and supply lines: cities collapse, states disintegrate, societies break down and populations die off, throwing humanity back to the pre-industrial age. Without transport animals, humanity would still be back in the Stone Age. No city or state can exist without transportation, for there can be no concentration of population and resources. No transportation means no extrahuman transportation, leaving walking and carrying as the only means of transit. This baseline determined the size and structure of human communities. Production was limited to what could be carried home, circumscribing the areas where humans could settle and colonise. Production capacity and resource availability kept communities small, forcing them to split and disperse. Because size dictates structure, small meant undifferentiated and egalitarian. Ultimately, the inherent limit of the physical capability of travel and portage formed a natural immunity to inequality: it kept communities small, egalitarian and autonomous, leaving their lands beyond conquest. As long as this constraint existed, communities resembled those of the Amazon Basin or the New Guinea Highlands. The domestication of animals and the invention of boats liberated humanity, making long-distance mobilisation possible. With this newfound capacity, communities no longer needed to fit their immediate environments; instead, they could build cities and integrate expansive areas – all roads lead to Rome. Transportation – not grain – is what brought the state and civilisation into existence, and what continues to maintain them. * Fugitive imagination
  Aeon | 13 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Wherever people are confined, displaced, silenced or made disposable, fugitive forms of knowledge and imagination emerge - by Shahram Khosravi Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Refugees at Moria Camp, Lesbos, Greece, July 2015. Photo by Georgios Makkas/Panos In 1986, I was 19 and behind bars in a small prison on the border between Iran and Pakistan. I had been arrested while attempting to cross the border irregularly into Pakistan. Everyone I met in that prison had been detained in connection with the border. It was a small space, the cells overcrowded and unsanitary, but it held an extraordinary diversity of people: local smugglers, large-scale drug-traffickers, undocumented migrant workers, and refugees; Iranians, Afghans, Pakistanis, as well as Baluch men whose communities had crossed these lands freely for generations but who were now being punished for violating the territorial order of the nation-state. Multiple languages were spoken at once. Different histories, geographies and ways of inhabiting the world converged within the same confined space. Yet we all shared one thing. We had refused, for different reasons, to respect the border. The state saw us through a single imaginary: as criminals, trespassers or illegal bodies to be contained. We, however, were animated by different imaginations. Each of us had imagined a life that exceeded the border and acted upon that imagination. In that sense, the prison did not simply contain border-crossers; it brought together people whose aspirations had collided with the territorial imaginary of the nation-state. That prison experience was perhaps one reason I became a scholar of borders. It remains, even today, the most important school of border studies I have ever attended. The conversations in the cells and during brief moments in the prison yard revolved around borders; how they operated, how they were enforced and, above all, how they might be circumvented. The prison became an unlikely site of knowledge-production: not simply the sharing of survival strategies but the enactment of an imagination that paid no heed to prison walls or state borders. M any years later, in a prison north of Stockholm, I interviewed a human smuggler who told me he saw himself as an architect: ‘I design a journey, like an architect designs a building,’ he said. The comparison stayed with me. Architects imagine and design structures that do not yet exist. Their work begins with the capacity to visualise what is not yet visible. In a similar way, smugglers design border crossings that, from the perspective of the state, would be impossible. A smuggler’s work consists of imagining routes where borders

As the home of Taiwan’s international airport as well as 2.2 million residents, Taoyuan must manage heavy truck traffic, air pollution, rapid population growth , and constant construction. Since March 2023, Taoyuan has run an AI-powered lung cancer screening initiative targeting citizens over 40 with risk factors that include occupational exposure and family history. Two years later, Taoyuan’s program had screened over 400,000 residents, detecting 233 lung cancer cases (90% in early stage), and achieving cost savings of NT$500 million ($17 million) through AI-aided diagnostics. To manage this volume without overwhelming local hospitals, the city deployed advanced AI software, which can predict cancer risk from a single low-dose CT scan without requiring additional clinical data, helping doctors to efficiently triage cases and focus resources on those most at risk. Modern AI-powered predictive models are being dangerously relied on in more decision-making arenas, including the delivery of government services, without justification. When residents complained about noise from illegally modified vehicle exhaust pipes, Taoyuan developed an AI system that analyzes exhaust pipe images and matches them against a database of legal configurations. Patrol cars equipped with cameras can now identify violations on sight, and complaints are down 20% over last year. Other AI-powered cameras monitor construction sites and factory chimneys for pollution, automatically triggering water sprays when dust levels rise too high, or alerting inspectors when chimney emissions change suspiciously. AI now optimizes traffic-light cycles at busy intersections, shaving about 10 seconds off each wait. * Why anything exists
  Aeon | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Here’s a new answer to the old question ‘why is there something rather than nothing?’ (It’s neither simple nor easy) - by Andrew T Brenner Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Icebergs (1893-95) by Robert E Peary. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York Writing in 1714, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz asked: ‘Why is there something rather than nothing?’ Philosophers have long wondered why the Universe exists, but Leibniz is credited with framing the issue in precisely these terms. The question is head-spinning when properly considered. This very obvious fact about the world – that things exist – can shift from seeming mundane and trivial to very surprising, even downright weird. Ludwig Wittgenstein expressed this astonishment when he wrote in his Tractatus (1921): ‘Not how the world is, is the mystical, but that it is.’ Arthur Schopenhauer in 1818 called the question ‘an unfathomable and always disquieting riddle’. And the Rigveda , one of the world’s oldest surviving religious texts, suggests that even the ‘overseer’ ( adhyakṣaḥ ) of the cosmos might not know the answer. Sheer existence is baffling. Philosophers continue to discuss the question today, but there isn’t any consensus about the answer. Some have suggested that it’s the sort of question that couldn’t possibly have an answer. In 1973, the German philosopher Carl Hempel wrote: His idea is that if you were trying to explain why, for instance, any penguins at all exist, then you couldn’t appeal to a penguin in your explanation – after all, that penguin is one of the things you are trying to explain. Similarly, if we’re looking for an explanation for why anything at all exists, then our explanation cannot appeal to an existing thing, since that existing thing is one of the things we are trying to explain. But Hempel thinks that there couldn’t possibly be any other sort of explanation for why anything exists, since any explanation for why something exists has to appeal to some other existing thing. One response to Hempel’s concern is to go ahead and do what he claimed can’t possibly be done: offer an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing, an explanation that ‘does not assume the existence of something’. Many philosophers have attempted to do just that. But there is something wrong with these explanations. W hy might we expect there to be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing in the first place? Leibniz thought there must be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing because, as he put it, nothing would have been so much ‘simpler and easier’ than something. It is this thought that lies behind much of the astonishment peo
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-new-answer-to-why-there-is-something-rather-than-nothing

  FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Ravenna Koenig | Longreads | November 12, 2026 | 19 minutes (5,133 words) In January, gravity was the only part of the physical world I recognized as I drove through Utqiaġvik for the first time. Day was one long night that slowly paled to powder blue, and turned pink before fading to black again. It was so cold the car tires squealed over the snow as if over Styrofoam. No part of Alaska lay farther north than this and the land was stark and flat: nothing to see in the distance, and no height to see the distance from. I was there to learn about loss. For thousands of years, a collar of sea ice, in some places feet thick and miles long, had hugged this northern shore most of the year. It protected the coastline from erosion, and served as a platform from which Iñupiat hunters pursued bowhead whales and other marine mammals; had for over a thousand years. But the ice was getting thinner, forming later, melting earlier. I’d come to report on this and other ways the Arctic landscape was bending to a warming atmosphere. I introduced myself to the mayor, whaling captains, wildlife biologists, the tribal government, and corporation heads. I said hi, I’m a journalist, I cover energy and environment, I just moved to Fairbanks, I’ll be coming up throughout the year. We talked about climate change, about the wild animals that ran like gold thread through the fabric of life there, about the oil that paid for almost everything: the roads, the fire department, the light and heat. The plane had landed in darkness, and the town was full of first-story windows. But driving along the thin road that separated the plywood-sided buildings from the frozen sea, I pulled over, and climbed up a snow berm that obscured it from view. The sight stilled me. The ice was snow-covered, unmoving, unending. Only snaking bands of rubble, left behind when motion stopped, distinguished it from the tundra. I didn’t know then what it would teach me about my expectations of continuity and stillness. I didn’t know it would tell me something about how to face a disappearing world. And I didn’t know the choice I’d made to come see it—to seek meaning and adventure at this vanishing outer edge of the country—would lead to losses of other kinds, other kinds of painful education. Her essay for the new issue of Virginia Quarterly Review wrestles with the impossibility of perfect vision and the inadvertent harms we visit on those who we most want to protect. Within months of Röntgen’s discovery, the unintended consequences of the X-ray began to emerge. In Tennessee, a boy came to the hospital after an accidental gunshot wound to his head. To test the equipment before trying it on his patient, his physician first conducted a prolonged X-ray of his own head. Three weeks later, the physician lost his hair. That same year, a New York doctor conducting X-ray experiments on himself noticed that his fingernails had stopped growing, his knuckles burned, and even the skin of his hand began to peel away. Blisters bloomed on other people’s bodies—on their hands and arms and chests. Thomas Edison started feeling cramps in his stomach; his vision began to blur. His glassblower, Clarence Dally, tested the quality of the fluoroscope tubes through daily X-rays of his own body. His hair started falling out, and his eyelashes and eyebrows burned away. His hands became red, swollen, and ulcerated. Knobs of deformed tissue bulged from his fingers. A biopsy of Dally’s pinky revealed cancer. . . . When Edison saw Dally’s tumors, he swore to never again expose himself or anyone else to radiation. In the year before Dally’s death, as the doomed glassblower lost himself to the disease, the New York World ran a story on the gruesome costs of this celebrated breakthrough. “Don’t talk to me about X-rays,” the prolific inventor told the reporter. “I am afraid of them.” “Meeting your kids in the Forgotten Realms.” “On cartoons, colors, ferris wheels, Father’s Day, Prince, coming out, the internet, and me.” “The first time I try to visit the devil, I bring my son.”

Syrians’ identification with Palestine was hardly new. What was new was that the chant came from soldiers of the new Syrian army, marching through the capital before a leader whose own political trajectory had begun in jihadist networks. Among them were soldiers in uniform, standing shoulder to shoulder with civilians, bowing and praying with them. A Damascene woman who had entered the mosque for the first time since Assad’s fall was astonished by what she saw. “We would never have imagined in our lifetime seeing our army praying side by side with civilians,” she told me, “and seeing soldiers praying in public.” To me, her surprise makes sense against the contentious history of state and religion during the Assad era.   FULL TEXT: We cross the valley, and the mountain pass reaches a plateau. We find ourselves in a field that stretches from one horizon to another, surrounded by mountains. It is a harsh environment that contrasts with the flourishing, ripe green fields that spread out before us. “They are the same, apples and hashish,” Ahmad tells us, as we move through the apple orchard toward the cannabis field — his land, inherited from his father, who inherited it from his grandfather. The same, yet six years after Lebanon legalized cannabis cultivation, Ahmad is still growing outside the law. In 2020, the Lebanese parliament passed a law legalizing the cultivation, processing and trade of cannabis, albeit strictly for medical, pharmaceutical and industrial purposes, under regulation by the state. It did not legalize recreational cannabis, the most common and sought-after form in Lebanon. The farmers have continued cultivating outside the legal system into 2026, because the legal framework was still absent and they felt unable to wait for the law to catch up with the reality of their lives. In the meantime, the region remains largely outside of state control, its cannabis farmers living in a twilight legal zone as outlaws, and as time goes on they increasingly find themselves having to compete or ally with bigger drug dealers who traffic in more profitable drugs. Like the land and dirt under our feet, Ahmad learned the cultivation of cannabis from his father. He also inherited the apple trees planted by his grandfather. He says this year he needs to cut some of them; they are no longer profitable, and he wants more space for the new greenhouses of marijuana — a complement to hashish, but not an alternative, he says. On July 17, 2025, the Regulatory Authority for the Cultivation of Cannabis was formed, with a president and board appointed by the Lebanese Cabinet. At the time of writing, even though the licensing platform has been built, it cannot be activated because the regulations and decrees necessary for implementation are still absent. Important questions still exist about how licensing will work: Who will benefit from the licenses? Will all farmers, big and small, be able to obtain them and competitively enter the market?
